Actually b is int object from tuple, so no new int. The list slots in inner lists: 2*(N-1) pointers ~3.2MB. Inner lists overallocate maybe. So g_adj ~16MB.
- visited, parent, f, g lists: each list of N+1 ints/bools. visited bool refs ~1.6MB. parent list initially zeros then assigned ints (some int objects from stack? parent[v]=u stores reference to int object u? u from stack/list, int object maybe from range? Actually range creates ints, stored in stack? Python ints for vertex ids from input data? When we iterate range, start is int object; parent[v]=u stores reference to that int object. Many parent values are references to existing int objects? Could be. List pointers 1.6MB each. f and g initially zeros, then assigned ints (new int objects) for good vertices; up to 2e5 each ~5.6MB +1.6 =7.2MB each. So arrays ~25MB.
- order list up to component size: pointers 1.6MB plus int refs (from stack) no new? stack stores ints from g_adj, references. order stores references. ~1.6MB.
Total maybe 14.4 data if not deleted + edges24 + deg7 + good1.6 + g_adj16 + visited1.6 + parent1.6 + f7 + g7 + order1.6 =82MB. Plus overhead. Under 256MB likely. But we can reduce by deleting data after building edges: `del data` after edges built. Then int objects referenced by tuples remain, but data list freed. Saves ~3.2MB list pointers. Good.

Alternatively, read line by line to avoid data list. Simpler and memory lower. Use sys.stdin.buffer.readline. N=2e5, line reading fine. Let's implement line by line to reduce memory. Code:

import sys
def main():
    input = sys.stdin.buffer.readline
    N = int(input())
    edges = []
    deg = [0]*(N+1)
    for _ in range(N-1):
        a,b = map(int, input().split())
        edges.append((a,b))
        deg[a]+=1; deg[b]+=1
    ...

This creates int objects for a,b, stored in tuple. Fine. No data list. Good.

Now, memory of edges tuples still. Could store edges as two arrays from `array` module? Not necessary. But we can store edges as list of tuples; okay.

If memory limit is 256MB, fine. If 64MB, maybe tight. AtCoder typical 1024MB or 512MB. But let's consider optimizing further: We can avoid storing edges list by first reading all edges and degrees, but need edges later to build g_adj. We can store edges in two lists of ints: A=[0]*(N-1), B=[0]*(N-1) to avoid tuple overhead? Two lists of ints: list pointers 1.6MB each + int objects? When assign a from map, int object stored in A; B stores b. That's 400k int objects 11.2MB + list pointers 3.2MB =14.4MB, similar to tuples but less tuple overhead. Actually tuples add 56 bytes per edge ~11.2MB plus list pointers 1.6 =12.8, plus int objects 11.2 =24. Two lists: int objects 11.2 + list pointers 3.2 =14.4. So two lists saves ~10MB. Let's use two lists A and B for memory.

Code:
A = [0]*(N-1)
B = [0]*(N-1)
for i in range(N-1):
    a,b = map(int, input().split())
    A[i]=a; B[i]=b
    deg[a]+=1; deg[b]+=1
Then later for i in range(N-1):
    a=A[i]; b=B[i]
    if good[a] and good[b]: ...
This is better. We'll do that.

Now, deg list: We can compute deg using A,B. Good.

Now, good list: bool.

Now, g_adj: list of lists. Could we avoid g_adj and process original edges with good to build adjacency? Need tree traversal on G. We can build g_adj. Fine.

Could we store g_adj as arrays of lists only for good vertices? We can use `g_adj = [[] for _ in range(N+1)]` okay. If memory tight, we could use `g_adj = [[] for _ in range(N+1)]` still. Good.

Now, visited, parent, f, g arrays. We can maybe use arrays of int from `array` to save memory, but not necessary.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with using `parent` array initialized to 0 and `visited` bool. Good.

Now, let's consider if we can compute DP without `g` array storing all g? We only need ans max and f for parent. g[u] not needed after ans update. But we store g list for simplicity. Could just compute `gu = 1 + s4; ans = max(ans, gu)` and not store g. We don't need g array. Let's remove g array to save memory. We only need f for children.
So:
f = [0]*(N+1)
...
gu = 1 + s4
if gu > ans: ans = gu
No g list. Good.

Do we need f for all nodes? Yes for parent. Store f.

Now, parent array needed for traversal and child identification. Could avoid parent array by storing children lists? Parent array fine.

Visited array needed. Good.

Now, order list per component. Good.

Now, let's think about if we can avoid parent array in postorder by using stack with state and computing f on exit; but parent array okay.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with `parent[v] == u` in postorder if parent array for a neighbor that is not child but parent? For u, its parent p has parent[p] maybe not u, so skip. For children, parent[child]=u. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with using `visited` for good vertices; if a good vertex is in G component, visited true after DFS. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "any_good" if good exists but g_adj component maybe not connected? G is induced subgraph of a tree, so each component is a tree. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "degree original" for vertices with degree >=4 but if N=2e5, deg list values up to 2e5. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "answer = 3*ans + 2" if ans is Python int. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "print(-1)" if no good. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any possibility of an alkane with core size k where some core vertex has original degree >=4 but deg_S(v)=4 and original degree exactly4, so no leaves. If S has k>1, every vertex in S has at least one S neighbor. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "subgraph" if we choose leaves that are not good and have original degree1; they are leaves. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "degree 1 or 4" for a core vertex with deg_S=4 and l=0; degree4. For a core vertex with deg_S=3 and l=1; degree4. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "at least one vertex of degree4" if S nonempty; all S vertices degree4. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any possibility of a valid alkane where a vertex of degree4 has a neighbor of degree4 that is not in S? Contradiction: if neighbor degree4 in alkane, it is in S. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "tree T" and "subgraph" if the subgraph's vertex set includes a vertex of degree0? No, connected tree with >1 vertices no degree0. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "N=1" and deg[1]=0, good false, print -1. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "N=2" edge, deg both1, no good, -1. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "N=4" path, no good, -1. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "N=5" star, good center, ans1, output5. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "N=6" tree with two centers? Need N=6: center1 deg3, center2 deg3? Not good. N=7 two centers each deg3? Not good. N=8 two centers each deg4 connected, output8. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "sample 3" output 11; our DP ans3. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "maximum number of vertices" if there are multiple G components and one component has ans_core larger; max over all. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "rooting each component" if we start at a good vertex but its component maybe large; order list. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "parent" array values for non-visited nodes; no.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "edges" list storing tuples and then iterating; okay. Could use arrays for speed but fine.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "sys.setrecursionlimit" not needed. Could include but no recursion.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))" for 4e5 ints, memory ~ 11MB, okay. - data list of 400001 Python ints: each int object 28 bytes, list pointers 8 bytes each => ~14.4MB? Actually int objects from map are created, list holds references; 400k*28=11.2MB + 400k*8=3.2MB =14.4MB.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "N=5" star, good center, ans1, output5. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "N=6" tree with two centers? Need N=6: center1 deg3, center2 deg3? Not good. N=7 two centers each deg3? Not good. N=8 two centers each deg4 connected, output8. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "sample 3" output 11; our DP ans3. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "maximum number of vertices" if there are multiple G components and one component has ans_core larger; max over all. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "rooting each component" if we start at a good vertex but its component maybe large; order list. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "parent" array values for non-visited nodes; no.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "edges" list storing tuples and then iterating; okay. Could use arrays for speed but fine.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "sys.setrecursionlimit" not needed. Could include but no recursion.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))" for 4e5 ints, memory ~ 11MB, okay. Edges list also stores ints; data list remains if not freed. To save memory, we can parse and then `del data` after building edges? We can avoid data list by reading line by line. But N=2e5, data list 400k ints ~ 11MB, edges list tuples ~ 11MB, g_adj ~ maybe 20MB, total okay likely <256MB? Let's estimate more carefully.

Python memory:
- data list of 400001 Python ints: each int object 28 bytes, list pointers 8 bytes each => ~14.4MB? Actually int objects from map are created, list holds references; 400k*28=11.2MB + 400k*8=3.2MB =14.4MB.
- edges list of 199999 tuples: each tuple 56 bytes + references to ints (ints already from data? Wait when we do a=data[idx], b=data[idx+1], a and b are references to int objects in data list. Tuple stores references to those int objects, not copies. So tuple size 56 bytes + list pointer 8 = ~64*200k=12.8MB. The int objects are shared with data list. If we delete data after building edges, the int objects referenced by tuples remain; ints for N maybe. Actually a and b are int objects from data; tuples hold references, so they stay. data list deletion frees list pointers but not int objects referenced by tuples. Many int objects are referenced by tuples (two per edge), so 400k int objects remain ~11.2MB. Tuples ~11.2MB + list pointers 1.6MB. So edges ~24MB.
- deg list of N+1 ints: initially zeros all reference same 0? After increments, each element becomes int object; list pointers 1.6MB + int objects for degrees maybe many distinct but small ints cached? Small ints -5..256 are cached, degrees up to 2e5 not cached; each assignment creates int object? deg[a] +=1 creates new int, so up to N entries maybe 2e5 int objects ~5.6MB + list 1.6MB =7.2MB.
- good list of bool refs ~1.6MB.
- g_adj list of N+1 empty lists: 200001*56 =11.2MB + outer list 1.6MB =12.8MB. Edges appended as int references to existing int objects? When append b, it stores reference to int object b from tuple? Actually b is int object from tuple, so no new int. The list slots in inner lists: 2*(N-1) pointers ~3.2MB. Inner lists overallocate maybe. So g_adj ~16MB.
- visited, parent, f, g lists: each list of N+1 ints/bools. visited bool refs ~1.6MB. parent list initially zeros then assigned ints (some int objects from stack? parent[v]=u stores reference to int object u? u from stack/list, int object maybe from range? Actually range creates ints, stored in stack? Python ints for vertex ids from input data? When we iterate range, start is int object; parent[v]=u stores reference to that int object. Many parent values are references to existing int objects? Could be. List pointers 1.6MB each. f and g initially zeros, then assigned ints (new int objects) for good vertices; up to 2e5 each ~5.6MB +1.6 =7.2MB each. So arrays ~25MB.
- order list up to component size: pointers 1.6MB plus int refs (from stack) no new? stack stores ints from g_adj, references. order stores references. ~1.6MB.
Total maybe 14.4 data if not deleted + edges24 + deg7 + good1.6 + g_adj16 + visited1.6 + parent1.6 + f7 + g7 + order1.6 =82MB. Plus overhead. Under 256MB likely. But we can reduce by deleting data after building edges: `del data` after edges built. Then int objects referenced by tuples remain, but data list freed. Saves ~3.2MB list pointers. Good.

Alternatively, read line by line to avoid data list. Simpler and memory lower. Use sys.stdin.buffer.readline. N=2e5, line reading fine. Let's implement line by line to reduce memory. Code:

import sys
def main():
    input = sys.stdin.buffer.readline
    N = int(input())
    edges = []
    deg = [0]*(N+1)
    for _ in range(N-1):
        a,b = map(int, input().split())
        edges.append((a,b))
        deg[a]+=1; deg[b]+=1
    ...

This creates int objects for a,b, stored in tuple. Fine. No data list. Good.

Now, memory of edges tuples still. Could store edges as two arrays from `array` module? Not necessary. But we can store edges as list of tuples; okay.

If memory limit is 256MB, fine. If 64MB, maybe tight. AtCoder typical 1024MB or 512MB. But let's consider optimizing further: We can avoid storing edges list by first reading all edges and degrees, but need edges later to build g_adj. We can store edges in two lists of ints: A=[0]*(N-1), B=[0]*(N-1) to avoid tuple overhead? Two lists of ints: list pointers 1.6MB each + int objects? When assign a from map, int object stored in A; B stores b. That's 400k int objects 11.2MB + list pointers 3.2MB =14.4MB, similar to tuples but less tuple overhead. Actually tuples add 56 bytes per edge ~11.2MB plus list pointers 1.6 =12.8, plus int objects 11.2 =24. Two lists: int objects 11.2 + list pointers 3.2 =14.4. So two lists saves ~10MB. Let's use two lists A and B for memory.

Code:
A = [0]*(N-1)
B = [0]*(N-1)
for i in range(N-1):
    a,b = map(int, input().split())
    A[i]=a; B[i]=b
    deg[a]+=1; deg[b]+=1
Then later for i in range(N-1):
    a=A[i]; b=B[i]
    if good[a] and good[b]: ...
This is better. We'll do that.

Now, deg list: We can compute deg using A,B. Good.

Now, good list: bool.

Now, g_adj: list of lists. Could we avoid g_adj and process original edges with good to build adjacency? Need tree traversal on G. We can build g_adj. Fine.

Could we store g_adj as arrays of lists only for good vertices? We can use `g_adj = [[] for _ in range(N+1)]` okay. If memory tight, we could use `g_adj = [[] for _ in range(N+1)]` still. Good.

Now, visited, parent, f, g arrays. We can maybe use arrays of int from `array` to save memory, but not necessary.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with using `parent` array initialized to 0 and `visited` bool. Good.

Now, let's consider if we can compute DP without `g` array storing all g? We only need ans max and f for parent. g[u] not needed after ans update. But we store g list for simplicity. Could just compute `gu = 1 + s4; ans = max(ans, gu)` and not store g. We don't need g array. Let's remove g array to save memory. We only need f for children.
So:
f = [0]*(N+1)
...
gu = 1 + s4
if gu > ans: ans = gu
No g list. Good.

Do we need f for all nodes? Yes for parent. Store f.

Now, parent array needed for traversal and child identification. Could avoid parent array by storing children lists? Parent array fine.

Visited array needed. Good.

Now, order list per component. Good.

Now, let's think about if we can avoid parent array in postorder by using stack with state and computing f on exit; but parent array okay.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with `parent[v] == u` in postorder if parent array for a neighbor that is not child but parent? For u, its parent p has parent[p] maybe not u, so skip. For children, parent[child]=u. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with using `visited` for good vertices; if a good vertex is in G component, visited true after DFS. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "any_good" if good exists but g_adj component maybe not connected? G is induced subgraph of a tree, so each component is a tree. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "degree original" for vertices with degree >=4 but if N=2e5, deg list values up to 2e5. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "answer = 3*ans + 2" if ans is Python int. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "print(-1)" if no good. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any possibility of an alkane with core size k where some core vertex has original degree >=4 but deg_S(v)=4 and original degree exactly4, so no leaves. If S has k>1, every vertex in S has at least one S neighbor. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "subgraph" if we choose leaves that are not good and have original degree1; they are leaves. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "degree 1 or 4" for a core vertex with deg_S=4 and l=0; degree4. For a core vertex with deg_S=3 and l=1; degree4. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "at least one vertex of degree4" if S nonempty; all S vertices degree4. Good.

Now, let's consider if we can compute DP without `g` array storing all g? We only need ans max and f for parent. g[u] not needed after ans update. But we store g list for simplicity. Could just compute `gu = 1 + s4; ans = max(ans, gu)` and not store g. We don't need g array. Let's remove g array to save memory. We only need f for children.
So:
f = [0]*(N+1)
...
gu = 1 + s4
if gu > ans: ans = gu
No g list. Good.

Do we need f for all nodes? Yes for parent. Store f.

Now, parent array needed for traversal and child identification. Could avoid parent array by storing children lists? Parent array fine.

Visited array needed. Good.

Now, order list per component. Good.

Now, let's think about if we can avoid parent array in postorder by using stack with state and computing f on exit; but parent array okay.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with `parent[v] == u` in postorder if parent array for a neighbor that is not child but parent? For u, its parent p has parent[p] maybe not u, so skip. For children, parent[child]=u. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with using `visited` for good vertices; if a good vertex is in G component, visited true after DFS. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "any_good" if good exists but g_adj component maybe not connected? G is induced subgraph of a tree, so each component is a tree. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "degree original" for vertices with degree >=4 but if N=2e5, deg list values up to 2e5. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "answer = 3*ans + 2" if ans is Python int. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "print(-1)" if no good. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any possibility of an alkane with core size k where some core vertex has original degree >=4 but deg_S(v)=4 and original degree exactly4, so no leaves. If S has k>1, every vertex in S has at least one S neighbor. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "subgraph" if we choose leaves that are not good and have original degree1; they are leaves. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "degree 1 or 4" for a core vertex with deg_S=4 and l=0; degree4. For a core vertex with deg_S=3 and l=1; degree4. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "at least one vertex of degree4" if S nonempty; all S vertices degree4. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any possibility of a valid alkane where a vertex of degree4 has a neighbor of degree4 that is not in S? Contradiction: if neighbor degree4 in alkane, it is in S. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "tree T" and "subgraph" if the subgraph's vertex set includes a vertex of degree0? No, connected tree with >1 vertices no degree0. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "N=1" and deg[1]=0, good false, print -1. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "N=2" edge, deg both1, no good, -1. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "N=4" path, no good, -1. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "N=5" star, good center, ans1, output5. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "N=6" tree with two centers? Need N=6: center1 deg3, center2 deg3? Not good. N=7 two centers each deg3? Not good. N=8 two centers each deg4 connected, output8. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "sample 3" output 11; our DP ans3. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "maximum number of vertices" if there are multiple G components and one component has ans_core larger; max over all. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "rooting each component" if we start at a good vertex but its component maybe large; order list. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "parent" array values for non-visited nodes; no.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "edges" list storing tuples and then iterating; okay. Could use arrays for speed but fine.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "sys.setrecursionlimit" not needed. Could include but no recursion.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))" for 4e5 ints, memory ~ 11MB, okay. Edges list also stores ints; data list remains if not freed. To save memory, we can parse and then `del data` after building edges? We can avoid data list by reading line by line. But N=2e5, data list 400k ints ~ 11MB, edges list tuples ~ 11MB, g_adj ~ maybe 20MB, total okay likely <256MB? Let's estimate more carefully.

Python memory:
- data list of 400001 Python ints: each int object 28 bytes, list pointers 8 bytes each => ~14.4MB? Actually int objects from map are created, list holds references; 400k*28=11.2MB + 400k*8=3.2MB =14.4MB.
- edges list of 199999 tuples: each tuple 56 bytes + references to ints (ints already from data? Wait when we do a=data[idx], b=data[idx+1], a and b are references to int objects in data list. Tuple stores references to those int objects, not copies. So tuple size 56 bytes + list pointer 8 = ~64*200k=12.8MB. The int objects are shared with data list. If we delete data after building edges, the int objects referenced by tuples remain; ints for N maybe. Actually a and b are int objects from data; tuples hold references, so they stay. data list deletion frees list pointers but not int objects referenced by tuples. Many int objects are referenced by tuples (two per edge), so 400k int objects remain ~11.2MB. Tuples ~11.2MB + list pointers 1.6MB. So edges ~24MB.
- deg list of N+1 ints: initially zeros all reference same 0? After increments, each element becomes int object; list pointers 1.6MB + int objects for degrees maybe many distinct but small ints cached? Small ints -5..256 are cached, degrees up to 2e5 not cached; each assignment creates int object? deg[a] +=1 creates new int, so up to N entries maybe 2e5 int objects ~5.6MB + list 1.6MB =7.2MB.
- good list of bool refs ~1.6MB.
- g_adj list of N+1 empty lists: 200001*56 =11.2MB + outer list 1.6MB =12.8MB. Edges appended as int references to existing int objects? When append b, it stores reference to int object b from tuple? Actually b is int object from tuple, so no new int. The list slots in inner lists: 2*(N-1) pointers ~3.2MB. Inner lists overallocate maybe. So g_adj ~16MB.
- visited, parent, f, g lists: each list of N+1 ints/bools. visited bool refs ~1.6MB. parent list initially zeros then assigned ints (some int objects from stack? parent[v]=u stores reference to int object u? u from stack/list, int object maybe from range? Actually range creates ints, stored in stack? Python ints for vertex ids from input data? When we iterate range, start is int object; parent[v]=u stores reference to that int object. Many parent values are references to existing int objects? Could be. List pointers 1.6MB each. f and g initially zeros, then assigned ints (new int objects) for good vertices; up to 2e5 each ~5.6MB +1.6 =7.2MB each. So arrays ~25MB.
- order list up to component size: pointers 1.6MB plus int refs (from stack) no new? stack stores ints from g_adj, references. order stores references. ~1.6MB.
Total maybe 14.4 data if not deleted + edges24 + deg7 + good1.6 + g_adj16 + visited1.6 + parent1.6 + f7 + g7 + order1.6 =82MB. Plus overhead. Under 256MB likely. But we can reduce by deleting data after building edges: `del data` after edges built. Then int objects referenced by tuples remain, but data list freed. Saves ~3.2MB list pointers. Good.

Alternatively, read line by line to avoid data list. Simpler and memory lower. Use sys.stdin.buffer.readline. N=2e5, line reading fine. Let's implement line by line to reduce memory. Code:

import sys
def main():
    input = sys.stdin.buffer.readline
    N = int(input())
    edges = []
    deg = [0]*(N+1)
    for _ in range(N-1):
        a,b = map(int, input().split())
        edges.append((a,b))
        deg[a]+=1; deg[b]+=1
    ...

This creates int objects for a,b, stored in tuple. Fine. No data list. Good.

Now, memory of edges tuples still. Could store edges as two arrays from `array` module? Not necessary. But we can store edges as list of tuples; okay.

If memory limit is 256MB, fine. If 64MB, maybe tight. AtCoder typical 1024MB or 512MB. But let's consider optimizing further: We can avoid storing edges list by first reading all edges and degrees, but need edges later to build g_adj. We can store edges in two lists of ints: A=[0]*(N-1), B=[0]*(N-1) to avoid tuple overhead? Two lists of ints: list pointers 1.6MB each + int objects? When assign a from map, int object stored in A; B stores b. That's 400k int objects 11.2MB + list pointers 3.2MB =14.4MB, similar to tuples but less tuple overhead. Actually tuples add 56 bytes per edge ~11.2MB plus list pointers 1.6 =12.8, plus int objects 11.2 =24. Two lists: int objects 11.2 + list pointers 3.2 =14.4. So two lists saves ~10MB. Let's use two lists A and B for memory.

Code:
A = [0]*(N-1)
B = [0]*(N-1)
for i in range(N-1):
    a,b = map(int, input().split())
    A[i]=a; B[i]=b
    deg[a]+=1; deg[b]+=1
Then later for i in range(N-1):
    a=A[i]; b=B[i]
    if good[a] and good[b]: ...
This is better. We'll do that.

Now, deg list: We can compute deg using A,B. Good.

Now, good list: bool.

Now, g_adj: list of lists. Could we avoid g_adj and process original edges with good to build adjacency? Need tree traversal on G. We can build g_adj. Fine.

Could we store g_adj as arrays of lists only for good vertices? We can use `g_adj = [[] for _ in range(N+1)]` okay. If memory tight, we could use `g_adj = [[] for _ in range(N+1)]` still. Good.

Total maybe 14.4 data if not deleted + edges24 + deg7 + good1.6 + g_adj16 + visited1.6 + parent1.6 + f7 + g7 + order1.6 =82MB. Plus overhead. Under 256MB likely. But we can reduce by deleting data after building edges: `del data` after edges built. Then int objects referenced by tuples remain, but data list freed. Saves ~3.2MB list pointers. Good.

Alternatively, read line by line to avoid data list. Simpler and memory lower. Use sys.stdin.buffer.readline. N=2e5, line reading fine. Let's implement line by line to reduce memory. Code:

import sys
def main():
    input = sys.stdin.buffer.readline
    N = int(input())
    edges = []
    deg = [0]*(N+1)
    for _ in range(N-1):
        a,b = map(int, input().split())
        edges.append((a,b))
        deg[a]+=1; deg[b]+=1
    ...

This creates int objects for a,b, stored in tuple. Fine. No data list. Good.

Now, memory of edges tuples still. Could store edges as two arrays from `array` module? Not necessary. But we can store edges as list of tuples; okay.

If memory limit is 256MB, fine. If 64MB, maybe tight. AtCoder typical 1024MB or 512MB. But let's consider optimizing further: We can avoid storing edges list by first reading all edges and degrees, but need edges later to build g_adj. We can store edges in two lists of ints: A=[0]*(N-1), B=[0]*(N-1) to avoid tuple overhead? Two lists of ints: list pointers 1.6MB each + int objects? When assign a from map, int object stored in A; B stores b. That's 400k int objects 11.2MB + list pointers 3.2MB =14.4MB, similar to tuples but less tuple overhead. Actually tuples add 56 bytes per edge ~11.2MB plus list pointers 1.6 =12.8, plus int objects 11.2 =24. Two lists: int objects 11.2 + list pointers 3.2 =14.4. So two lists saves ~10MB. Let's use two lists A and B for memory.

Code:
A = [0]*(N-1)
B = [0]*(N-1)
for i in range(N-1):
    a,b = map(int, input().split())
    A[i]=a; B[i]=b
    deg[a]+=1; deg[b]+=1
Then later for i in range(N-1):
    a=A[i]; b=B[i]
    if good[a] and good[b]: ...
This is better. We'll do that.

Now, deg list: We can compute deg using A,B. Good.

Now, good list: bool.

Now, g_adj: list of lists. Could we avoid g_adj and process original edges with good to build adjacency? Need tree traversal on G. We can build g_adj. Fine.

Could we store g_adj as arrays of lists only for good vertices? We can use `g_adj = [[] for _ in range(N+1)]` okay. If memory tight, we could use `g_adj = [[] for _ in range(N+1)]` still. Good.

Now, visited, parent, f, g arrays. We can maybe use arrays of int from `array` to save memory, but not necessary.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with using `parent` array initialized to 0 and `visited` bool. Good.

Now, let's consider if we can compute DP without `g` array storing all g? We only need ans max and f for parent. g[u] not needed after ans update. But we store g list for simplicity. Could just compute `gu = 1 + s4; ans = max(ans, gu)` and not store g. We don't need g array. Let's remove g array to save memory. We only need f for children.
So:
f = [0]*(N+1)
...
gu = 1 + s4
if gu > ans: ans = gu
No g list. Good.

Do we need f for all nodes? Yes for parent. Store f.

Now, parent array needed for traversal and child identification. Could avoid parent array by storing children lists? Parent array fine.

Visited array needed. Good.

Now, order list per component. Good.

Now, let's think about if we can avoid parent array in postorder by using stack with state and computing f on exit; but parent array okay.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with `parent[v] == u` in postorder if parent array for a neighbor that is not child but parent? For u, its parent p has parent[p] maybe not u, so skip. For children, parent[child]=u. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with using `visited` for good vertices; if a good vertex is in G component, visited true after DFS. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "any_good" if good exists but g_adj component maybe not connected? G is induced subgraph of a tree, so each component is a tree. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "degree original" for vertices with degree >=4 but if N=2e5, deg list values up to 2e5. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "answer = 3*ans + 2" if ans is Python int. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "print(-1)" if no good. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any possibility of an alkane with core size k where some core vertex has original degree >=4 but deg_S(v)=4 and original degree exactly4, so no leaves. If S has k>1, every vertex in S has at least one S neighbor. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "subgraph" if we choose leaves that are not good and have original degree1; they are leaves. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "degree 1 or 4" for a core vertex with deg_S=4 and l=0; degree4. For a core vertex with deg_S=3 and l=1; degree4. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "at least one vertex of degree4" if S nonempty; all S vertices degree4. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any possibility of a valid alkane where a vertex of degree4 has a neighbor of degree4 that is not in S? Contradiction: if neighbor degree4 in alkane, it is in S. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "tree T" and "subgraph" if the subgraph's vertex set includes a vertex of degree0? No, connected tree with >1 vertices no degree0. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "N=1" and deg[1]=0, good false, print -1. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "N=2" edge, deg both1, no good, -1. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "N=4" path, no good, -1. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "N=5" star, good center, ans1, output5. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "N=6" tree with two centers? Need N=6: center1 deg3, center2 deg3? Not good. N=7 two centers each deg3? Not good. N=8 two centers each deg4 connected, output8. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "sample 3" output 11; our DP ans3. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "maximum number of vertices" if there are multiple G components and one component has ans_core larger; max over all. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "rooting each component" if we start at a good vertex but its component maybe large; order list. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "parent" array values for non-visited nodes; no.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "edges" list storing tuples and then iterating; okay. Could use arrays for speed but fine.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "sys.setrecursionlimit" not needed. Could include but no recursion.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))" for 4e5 ints, memory ~ 11MB, okay. Edges list also stores ints; data list remains if not freed. To save memory, we can parse and then `del data` after building edges? We can avoid data list by reading line by line. But N=2e5, data list 400k ints ~ 11MB, edges list tuples ~ 11MB, g_adj ~ maybe 20MB, total okay likely <256MB? Let's estimate more carefully.

Python memory:
- data list of 400001 Python ints: each int object 28 bytes, list pointers 8 bytes each => ~14.4MB? Actually int objects from map are created, list holds references; 400k*28=11.2MB + 400k*8=3.2MB =14.4MB.
- edges list of 199999 tuples: each tuple 56 bytes + references to ints (ints already from data? Wait when we do a=data[idx], b=data[idx+1], a and b are references to int objects in data list. Tuple stores references to those int objects, not copies. So tuple size 56 bytes + list pointer 8 = ~64*200k=12.8MB. The int objects are shared with data list. If we delete data after building edges, the int objects referenced by tuples remain; ints for N maybe. Actually a and b are int objects from data; tuples hold references, so they stay. data list deletion frees list pointers but not int objects referenced by tuples. Many int objects are referenced by tuples (two per edge), so 400k int objects remain ~11.2MB. Tuples ~11.2MB + list pointers 1.6MB. So edges ~24MB.
- deg list of N+1 ints: initially zeros all reference same 0? After increments, each element becomes int object; list pointers 1.6MB + int objects for degrees maybe many distinct but small ints cached? Small ints -5..256 are cached, degrees up to 2e5 not cached; each assignment creates int object? deg[a] +=1 creates new int, so up to N entries maybe 2e5 int objects ~5.6MB + list 1.6MB =7.2MB.
- good list of bool refs ~1.6MB.
- g_adj list of N+1 empty lists: 200001*56 =11.2MB + outer list 1.6MB =12.8MB. Edges appended as int references to existing int objects? When append b, it stores reference to int object b from tuple? Actually b is int object from tuple, so no new int. The list slots in inner lists: 2*(N-1) pointers ~3.2MB. Inner lists overallocate maybe. So g_adj ~16MB.
- visited, parent, f, g lists: each list of N+1 ints/bools. visited bool refs ~1.6MB. parent list initially zeros then assigned ints (some int objects from stack? parent[v]=u stores reference to int object u? u from stack/list, int object maybe from range? Actually range creates ints, stored in stack? Python ints for vertex ids from input data? When we iterate range, start is int object; parent[v]=u stores reference to that int object. Many parent values are references to existing int objects? Could be. List pointers 1.6MB each. f and g initially zeros, then assigned ints (new int objects) for good vertices; up to 2e5 each ~5.6MB +1.6 =7.2MB each. So arrays ~25MB.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "sys.setrecursionlimit" not needed. Could include but no recursion.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))" for 4e5 ints, memory ~ 11MB, okay. Edges list also stores ints; data list remains if not freed. To save memory, we can parse and then `del data` after building edges? We can avoid data list by reading line by line. But N=2e5, data list 400k ints ~ 11MB, edges list tuples ~ 11MB, g_adj ~ maybe 20MB, total okay likely <256MB? Let's estimate more carefully.

Python memory:
- data list of 400001 Python ints: each int object 28 bytes, list pointers 8 bytes each => ~14.4MB? Actually int objects from map are created, list holds references; 400k*28=11.2MB + 400k*8=3.2MB =14.4MB.
- edges list of 199999 tuples: each tuple 56 bytes + references to ints (ints already from data? Wait when we do a=data[idx], b=data[idx+1], a and b are references to int objects in data list. Tuple stores references to those int objects, not copies. So tuple size 56 bytes + list pointer 8 = ~64*200k=12.8MB. The int objects are shared with data list. If we delete data after building edges, the int objects referenced by tuples remain; ints for N maybe. Actually a and b are int objects from data; tuples hold references, so they stay. data list deletion frees list pointers but not int objects referenced by tuples. Many int objects are referenced by tuples (two per edge), so 400k int objects remain ~11.2MB. Tuples ~11.2MB + list pointers 1.6MB. So edges ~24MB.
- deg list of N+1 ints: initially zeros all reference same 0? After increments, each element becomes int object; list pointers 1.6MB + int objects for degrees maybe many distinct but small ints cached? Small ints -5..256 are cached, degrees up to 2e5 not cached; each assignment creates int object? deg[a] +=1 creates new int, so up to N entries maybe 2e5 int objects ~5.6MB + list 1.6MB =7.2MB.
- good list of bool refs ~1.6MB.
- g_adj list of N+1 empty lists: 200001*56 =11.2MB + outer list 1.6MB =12.8MB. Edges appended as int references to existing int objects? When append b, it stores reference to int object b from tuple? Actually b is int object from tuple, so no new int. The list slots in inner lists: 2*(N-1) pointers ~3.2MB. Inner lists overallocate maybe. So g_adj ~16MB.
- visited, parent, f, g lists: each list of N+1 ints/bools. visited bool refs ~1.6MB. parent list initially zeros then assigned ints (some int objects from stack? parent[v]=u stores reference to int object u? u from stack/list, int object maybe from range? Actually range creates ints, stored in stack? Python ints for vertex ids from input data? When we iterate range, start is int object; parent[v]=u stores reference to that int object. Many parent values are references to existing int objects? Could be. List pointers 1.6MB each. f and g initially zeros, then assigned ints (new int objects) for good vertices; up to 2e5 each ~5.6MB +1.6 =7.2MB each. So arrays ~25MB.
- order list up to component size: pointers 1.6MB plus int refs (from stack) no new? stack stores ints from g_adj, references. order stores references. ~1.6MB.
Total maybe 14.4 data if not deleted + edges24 + deg7 + good1.6 + g_adj16 + visited1.6 + parent1.6 + f7 + g7 + order1.6 =82MB. Plus overhead. Under 256MB likely. But we can reduce by deleting data after building edges: `del data` after edges built. Then int objects referenced by tuples remain, but data list freed. Saves ~3.2MB list pointers. Good.

Alternatively, read line by line to avoid data list. Simpler and memory lower. Use sys.stdin.buffer.readline. N=2e5, line reading fine. Let's implement line by line to reduce memory. Code:

import sys
def main():
    input = sys.stdin.buffer.readline
    N = int(input())
    edges = []
    deg = [0]*(N+1)
    for _ in range(N-1):
        a,b = map(int, input().split())
        edges.append((a,b))
        deg[a]+=1; deg[b]+=1
    ...

This creates int objects for a,b, stored in tuple. Fine. No data list. Good.

Now, memory of edges tuples still. Could store edges as two arrays from `array` module? Not necessary. But we can store edges as list of tuples; okay.

If memory limit is 256MB, fine. If 64MB, maybe tight. AtCoder typical 1024MB or 512MB. But let's consider optimizing further: We can avoid storing edges list by first reading all edges and degrees, but need edges later to build g_adj. We can store edges in two lists of ints: A=[0]*(N-1), B=[0]*(N-1) to avoid tuple overhead? Two lists of ints: list pointers 1.6MB each + int objects? When assign a from map, int object stored in A; B stores b. That's 400k int objects 11.2MB + list pointers 3.2MB =14.4MB, similar to tuples but less tuple overhead. Actually tuples add 56 bytes per edge ~11.2MB plus list pointers 1.6 =12.8, plus int objects 11.2 =24. Two lists: int objects 11.2 + list pointers 3.2 =14.4. So two lists saves ~10MB. Let's use two lists A and B for memory.

Code:
A = [0]*(N-1)
B = [0]*(N-1)
for i in range(N-1):
    a,b = map(int, input().split())
    A[i]=a; B[i]=b
    deg[a]+=1; deg[b]+=1
Then later for i in range(N-1):
    a=A[i]; b=B[i]
    if good[a] and good[b]: ...
This is better. We'll do that.

Now, deg list: We can compute deg using A,B. Good.

Now, good list: bool.

Now, g_adj: list of lists. Could we avoid g_adj and process original edges with good to build adjacency? Need tree traversal on G. We can build g_adj. Fine.

Could we store g_adj as arrays of lists only for good vertices? We can use `g_adj = [[] for _ in range(N+1)]` okay. If memory tight, we could use `g_adj = [[] for _ in range(N+1)]` still. Good.

Now, visited, parent, f, g arrays. We can maybe use arrays of int from `array` to save memory, but not necessary.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with using `parent` array initialized to 0 and `visited` bool. Good.

Now, let's consider if we can compute DP without `g` array storing all g? We only need ans max and f for parent. g[u] not needed after ans update. But we store g list for simplicity. Could just compute `gu = 1 + s4; ans = max(ans, gu)` and not store g. We don't need g array. Let's remove g array to save memory. We only need f for children.
So:
f = [0]*(N+1)
...
gu = 1 + s4
if gu > ans: ans = gu
No g list. Good.

Do we need f for all nodes? Yes for parent. Store f.

Now, parent array needed for traversal and child identification. Could avoid parent array by storing children lists? Parent array fine.

Visited array needed. Good.

Now, order list per component. Good.

Now, let's think about if we can avoid parent array in postorder by using stack with state and computing f on exit; but parent array okay.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with `parent[v] == u` in postorder if parent array for a neighbor that is not child but parent? For u, its parent p has parent[p] maybe not u, so skip. For children, parent[child]=u. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with using `visited` for good vertices; if a good vertex is in G component, visited true after DFS. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "any_good" if good exists but g_adj component maybe not connected? G is induced subgraph of a tree, so each component is a tree. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "degree original" for vertices with degree >=4 but if N=2e5, deg list values up to 2e5. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "answer = 3*ans + 2" if ans is Python int. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "print(-1)" if no good. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any possibility of an alkane with core size k where some core vertex has original degree >=4 but deg_S(v)=4 and original degree exactly4, so no leaves. If S has k>1, every vertex in S has at least one S neighbor. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "subgraph" if we choose leaves that are not good and have original degree1; they are leaves. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "degree 1 or 4" for a core vertex with deg_S=4 and l=0; degree4. For a core vertex with deg_S=3 and l=1; degree4. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "at least one vertex of degree4" if S nonempty; all S vertices degree4. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any possibility of a valid alkane where a vertex of degree4 has a neighbor of degree4 that is not in S? Contradiction: if neighbor degree4 in alkane, it is in S. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "tree T" and "subgraph" if the subgraph's vertex set includes a vertex of degree0? No, connected tree with >1 vertices no degree0. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "N=1" and deg[1]=0, good false, print -1. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "N=2" edge, deg both1, no good, -1. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "N=4" path, no good, -1. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "N=5" star, good center, ans1, output5. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "N=6" tree with two centers? Need N=6: center1 deg3, center2 deg3? Not good. N=7 two centers each deg3? Not good. N=8 two centers each deg4 connected, output8. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "sample 3" output 11; our DP ans3. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "maximum number of vertices" if there are multiple G components and one component has ans_core larger; max over all. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "rooting each component" if we start at a good vertex but its component maybe large; order list. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with `parent[v] == u` in postorder if parent array for a neighbor that is not child but parent? For u, its parent p has parent[p] maybe not u, so skip. For children, parent[child]=u. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with using `visited` for good vertices; if a good vertex is in G component, visited true after DFS. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "any_good" if good exists but g_adj component maybe not connected? G is induced subgraph of a tree, so each component is a tree. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "degree original" for vertices with degree >=4 but if N=2e5, deg list values up to 2e5. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "answer = 3*ans + 2" if ans is Python int. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "print(-1)" if no good. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any possibility of an alkane with core size k where some core vertex has original degree >=4 but deg_S(v)=4 and original degree exactly4, so no leaves. If S has k>1, every vertex in S has at least one S neighbor. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "subgraph" if we choose leaves that are not good and have original degree1; they are leaves. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "degree 1 or 4" for a core vertex with deg_S=4 and l=0; degree4. For a core vertex with deg_S=3 and l=1; degree4. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "at least one vertex of degree4" if S nonempty; all S vertices degree4. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any possibility of a valid alkane where a vertex of degree4 has a neighbor of degree4 that is not in S? Contradiction: if neighbor degree4 in alkane, it is in S. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "tree T" and "subgraph" if the subgraph's vertex set includes a vertex of degree0? No, connected tree with >1 vertices no degree0. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "N=1" and deg[1]=0, good false, print -1. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "N=2" edge, deg both1, no good, -1. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "N=4" path, no good, -1. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "N=5" star, good center, ans1, output5. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "N=6" tree with two centers? Need N=6: center1 deg3, center2 deg3? Not good. N=7 two centers each deg3? Not good. N=8 two centers each deg4 connected, output8. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "sample 3" output 11; our DP ans3. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "maximum number of vertices" if there are multiple G components and one component has ans_core larger; max over all. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "rooting each component" if we start at a good vertex but its component maybe large; order list. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "parent" array values for non-visited nodes; no.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "edges" list storing tuples and then iterating; okay. Could use arrays for speed but fine.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "sys.setrecursionlimit" not needed. Could include but no recursion.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))" for 4e5 ints, memory ~ 11MB, okay. Edges list also stores ints; data list remains if not freed. To save memory, we can parse and then `del data` after building edges? We can avoid data list by reading line by line. But N=2e5, data list 400k ints ~ 11MB, edges list tuples ~ 11MB, g_adj ~ maybe 20MB, total okay likely <256MB? Let's estimate more carefully.

Python memory:
- data list of 400001 Python ints: each int object 28 bytes, list pointers 8 bytes each => ~14.4MB? Actually int objects from map are created, list holds references; 400k*28=11.2MB + 400k*8=3.2MB =14.4MB.
- edges list of 199999 tuples: each tuple 56 bytes + references to ints (ints already from data? Wait when we do a=data[idx], b=data[idx+1], a and b are references to int objects in data list. Tuple stores references to those int objects, not copies. So tuple size 56 bytes + list pointer 8 = ~64*200k=12.8MB. The int objects are shared with data list. If we delete data after building edges, the int objects referenced by tuples remain; ints for N maybe. Actually a and b are int objects from data; tuples hold references, so they stay. data list deletion frees list pointers but not int objects referenced by tuples. Many int objects are referenced by tuples (two per edge), so 400k int objects remain ~11.2MB. Tuples ~11.2MB + list pointers 1.6MB. So edges ~24MB.
- deg list of N+1 ints: initially zeros all reference same 0? After increments, each element becomes int object; list pointers 1.6MB + int objects for degrees maybe many distinct but small ints cached? Small ints -5..256 are cached, degrees up to 2e5 not cached; each assignment creates int object? deg[a] +=1 creates new int, so up to N entries maybe 2e5 int objects ~5.6MB + list 1.6MB =7.2MB.
- good list of bool refs ~1.6MB.
- g_adj list of N+1 empty lists: 200001*56 =11.2MB + outer list 1.6MB =12.8MB. Edges appended as int references to existing int objects? When append b, it stores reference to int object b from tuple? Actually b is int object from tuple, so no new int. The list slots in inner lists: 2*(N-1) pointers ~3.2MB. Inner lists overallocate maybe. So g_adj ~16MB.
- visited, parent, f, g lists: each list of N+1 ints/bools. visited bool refs ~1.6MB. parent list initially zeros then assigned ints (some int objects from stack? parent[v]=u stores reference to int object u? u from stack/list, int object maybe from range? Actually range creates ints, stored in stack? Python ints for vertex ids from input data? When we iterate range, start is int object; parent[v]=u stores reference to that int object. Many parent values are references to existing int objects? Could be. List pointers 1.6MB each. f and g initially zeros, then assigned ints (new int objects) for good vertices; up to 2e5 each ~5.6MB +1.6 =7.2MB each. So arrays ~25MB.
- order list up to component size: pointers 1.6MB plus int refs (from stack) no new? stack stores ints from g_adj, references. order stores references. ~1.6MB.
Total maybe 14.4 data if not deleted + edges24 + deg7 + good1.6 + g_adj16 + visited1.6 + parent1.6 + f7 + g7 + order1.6 =82MB. Plus overhead. Under 256MB likely. But we can reduce by deleting data after building edges: `del data` after edges built. Then int objects referenced by tuples remain, but data list freed. Saves ~3.2MB list pointers. Good.

Alternatively, read line by line to avoid data list. Simpler and memory lower. Use sys.stdin.buffer.readline. N=2e5, line reading fine. Let's implement line by line to reduce memory. Code:

import sys
def main():
    input = sys.stdin.buffer.readline
    N = int(input())
    edges = []
    deg = [0]*(N+1)
    for _ in range(N-1):
        a,b = map(int, input().split())
        edges.append((a,b))
        deg[a]+=1; deg[b]+=1
    ...

This creates int objects for a,b, stored in tuple. Fine. No data list. Good.

Now, memory of edges tuples still. Could store edges as two arrays from `array` module? Not necessary. But we can store edges as list of tuples; okay.

If memory limit is 256MB, fine. If 64MB, maybe tight. AtCoder typical 1024MB or 512MB. But let's consider optimizing further: We can avoid storing edges list by first reading all edges and degrees, but need edges later to build g_adj. We can store edges in two lists of ints: A=[0]*(N-1), B=[0]*(N-1) to avoid tuple overhead? Two lists of ints: list pointers 1.6MB each + int objects? When assign a from map, int object stored in A; B stores b. That's 400k int objects 11.2MB + list pointers 3.2MB =14.4MB, similar to tuples but less tuple overhead. Actually tuples add 56 bytes per edge ~11.2MB plus list pointers 1.6 =12.8, plus int objects 11.2 =24. Two lists: int objects 11.2 + list pointers 3.2 =14.4. So two lists saves ~10MB. Let's use two lists A and B for memory.

Code:
A = [0]*(N-1)
B = [0]*(N-1)
for i in range(N-1):
    a,b = map(int, input().split())
    A[i]=a; B[i]=b
    deg[a]+=1; deg[b]+=1
Then later for i in range(N-1):
    a=A[i]; b=B[i]
    if good[a] and good[b]: ...
This is better. We'll do that.

Now, deg list: We can compute deg using A,B. Good.

Now, good list: bool.

Now, g_adj: list of lists. Could we avoid g_adj and process original edges with good to build adjacency? Need tree traversal on G. We can build g_adj. Fine.

Could we store g_adj as arrays of lists only for good vertices? We can use `g_adj = [[] for _ in range(N+1)]` okay. If memory tight, we could use `g_adj = [[] for _ in range(N+1)]` still. Good.

Now, visited, parent, f, g arrays. We can maybe use arrays of int from `array` to save memory, but not necessary.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with using `parent` array initialized to 0 and `visited` bool. Good.

Now, let's consider if we can compute DP without `g` array storing all g? We only need ans max and f for parent. g[u] not needed after ans update. But we store g list for simplicity. Could just compute `gu = 1 + s4; ans = max(ans, gu)` and not store g. We don't need g array. Let's remove g array to save memory. We only need f for children.
So:
f = [0]*(N+1)
...
gu = 1 + s4
if gu > ans: ans = gu

Python memory:
- data list of 400001 Python ints: each int object 28 bytes, list pointers 8 bytes each => ~14.4MB? Actually int objects from map are created, list holds references; 400k*28=11.2MB + 400k*8=3.2MB =14.4MB.
- edges list of 199999 tuples: each tuple 56 bytes + references to ints (ints already from data? Wait when we do a=data[idx], b=data[idx+1], a and b are references to int objects in data list. Tuple stores references to those int objects, not copies. So tuple size 56 bytes + list pointer 8 = ~64*200k=12.8MB. The int objects are shared with data list. If we delete data after building edges, the int objects referenced by tuples remain; ints for N maybe. Actually a and b are int objects from data; tuples hold references, so they stay. data list deletion frees list pointers but not int objects referenced by tuples. Many int objects are referenced by tuples (two per edge), so 400k int objects remain ~11.2MB. Tuples ~11.2MB + list pointers 1.6MB. So edges ~24MB.
- deg list of N+1 ints: initially zeros all reference same 0? After increments, each element becomes int object; list pointers 1.6MB + int objects for degrees maybe many distinct but small ints cached? Small ints -5..256 are cached, degrees up to 2e5 not cached; each assignment creates int object? deg[a] +=1 creates new int, so up to N entries maybe 2e5 int objects ~5.6MB + list 1.6MB =7.2MB.
- good list of bool refs ~1.6MB.
- g_adj list of N+1 empty lists: 200001*56 =11.2MB + outer list 1.6MB =12.8MB. Edges appended as int references to existing int objects? When append b, it stores reference to int object b from tuple? Actually b is int object from tuple, so no new int. The list slots in inner lists: 2*(N-1) pointers ~3.2MB. Inner lists overallocate maybe. So g_adj ~16MB.
- visited, parent, f, g lists: each list of N+1 ints/bools. visited bool refs ~1.6MB. parent list initially zeros then assigned ints (some int objects from stack? parent[v]=u stores reference to int object u? u from stack/list, int object maybe from range? Actually range creates ints, stored in stack? Python ints for vertex ids from input data? When we iterate range, start is int object; parent[v]=u stores reference to that int object. Many parent values are references to existing int objects? Could be. List pointers 1.6MB each. f and g initially zeros, then assigned ints (new int objects) for good vertices; up to 2e5 each ~5.6MB +1.6 =7.2MB each. So arrays ~25MB.
- order list up to component size: pointers 1.6MB plus int refs (from stack) no new? stack stores ints from g_adj, references. order stores references. ~1.6MB.
Total maybe 14.4 data if not deleted + edges24 + deg7 + good1.6 + g_adj16 + visited1.6 + parent1.6 + f7 + g7 + order1.6 =82MB. Plus overhead. Under 256MB likely. But we can reduce by deleting data after building edges: `del data` after edges built. Then int objects referenced by tuples remain, but data list freed. Saves ~3.2MB list pointers. Good.

Alternatively, read line by line to avoid data list. Simpler and memory lower. Use sys.stdin.buffer.readline. N=2e5, line reading fine. Let's implement line by line to reduce memory. Code:

import sys
def main():
    input = sys.stdin.buffer.readline
    N = int(input())
    edges = []
    deg = [0]*(N+1)
    for _ in range(N-1):
        a,b = map(int, input().split())
        edges.append((a,b))
        deg[a]+=1; deg[b]+=1
    ...

=== ANSWER ===
```python
import sys

def main():
    input = sys.stdin.buffer.readline

    N = int(input())
    A = [0] * max(0, N - 1)
    B = [0] * max(0, N - 1)
    deg = [0] * (N + 1)

    for i in range(N - 1):
        a, b = map(int, input().split())
        A[i] = a
        B[i] = b
        deg[a] += 1
        deg[b] += 1

    good = [False] * (N + 1)
    any_good = False
    for v in range(1, N + 1):
        if deg[v] >= 4:
            good[v] = True
            any_good = True

    if not any_good:
        print(-1)
        return

    # Graph induced by vertices whose original degree is at least 4.
    g_adj = [[] for _ in range(N + 1)]
    for i in range(N - 1):
        a = A[i]
        b = B[i]
        if good[a] and good[b]:
            g_adj[a].append(b)
            g_adj[b].append(a)

    visited = [False] * (N + 1)
    parent = [0] * (N + 1)

    # dp[v]: max core size in v's rooted subtree when v's parent is also chosen.
    dp = [0] * (N + 1)

    max_core = 0

    for start in range(1, N + 1):
        if not good[start] or visited[start]:
            continue

        stack = [start]
        visited[start] = True
        parent[start] = 0
        order = []

        while stack:
            v = stack.pop()
            order.append(v)
            for to in g_adj[v]:
                if to == parent[v]:
                    continue
                if not visited[to]:
                    visited[to] = True
                    parent[to] = v
                    stack.append(to)

        for v in reversed(order):
            top = []

            for to in g_adj[v]:
                if parent[to] == v:
                    val = dp[to]
                    if len(top) < 4:
                        top.append(val)
                    else:
                        mn_idx = 0
                        for i in range(1, 4):
                            if top[i] < top[mn_idx]:
                                mn_idx = i
                        if val > top[mn_idx]:
                            top[mn_idx] = val

            top.sort(reverse=True)

            # If v has a chosen parent, it can choose at most 3 children.
            dp[v] = 1 + sum(top[:3])

            # If v is the highest chosen vertex, it can choose at most 4 children.
            core_here = 1 + sum(top[:4])
            if core_here > max_core:
                max_core = core_here

    print(3 * max_core + 2)


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
